The coupling flow of 𝒩 = 4 {\cal N}=\,4 super Yang–Mills theory maximilian.rupprecht@itp.uni-hannover.de
.

Abstract
We offer a novel perspective on 𝒩 = 4 {\cal N}=\,4 supersymmetric Yang–Mills (SYM) theory through the framework of the Nicolai map, a transformation of the bosonic fields that allows one to compute quantum correlators in terms of a free, purely bosonic functional measure. Generally, any Nicolai map is obtained through a path-ordered exponential of the so-called coupling flow operator. The latter can be canonically constructed in any gauge using an 𝒩 = 1 {\cal N}=\,1 off-shell superfield formulation of 𝒩 = 4 {\cal N}=\,4 SYM, or alternatively through dimensional reduction of the result from 𝒩 = 1 {\cal N}=\,1 D = 10 D=10 SYM, in which case we need to restrict to the Landau gauge. We propose a general theory of the 𝒩 = 4 {\cal N}=\,4 coupling flow operator, arguing that it exhibits an ambiguity in form of an R-symmetry freedom given by the Lie algebra 𝔰 ​ 𝔲 ​ ( 4 ) \mathfrak{su}(4) . This theory incorporates our two construction approaches as special points in 𝔰 ​ 𝔲 ​ ( 4 ) \mathfrak{su}(4) and defines a broad class of Nicolai maps for 𝒩 = 4 {\cal N}=\,4 SYM.

MnLargeSymbols’171

1
Introduction

1.1
History and overview

Gauge theories are our best candidates for describing Nature at elementary scales. Out of all such theories, maximally supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory in four dimensions (
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM) takes on a special role. In a sense it is the most simple (possibly integrable) gauge theory that can be formulated. Indeed it has been a long standing goal of theoretical physicists to solve this particular theory analytically. While being a toy model, it is expected that progress on this matter would have a drastic impact on our understanding of more complex models of nature. It is therefore important to continue this pursuit and investigate
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM from every possible angle.

A so far relatively unknown approach to supersymmetric field theories is referred to as the Nicolai map. It is based on works from Nicolai, Dietz, Flume and Lechtenfeld
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
]
from the 1980s. The Nicolai map is a transformation of the bosonic Yang–Mills fields that relates the interacting theory at some coupling
g
to the free theory at zero coupling. It allows one to compute quantum correlators in terms of a free, purely bosonic functional measure, entirely bypassing the use of any anticommuting (Grassmann) variables. After a pause of roughly 35 years there has recently been renewed interest on this matter starting with the papers
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
. Shortly after, a general formula for the Nicolai map in terms of the so-called coupling flow operator
R_{g}
was found in
[
12
]
. For theories that have an off-shell superfield formalism the coupling flow operator can be constructed canonically. In these cases, a general construction method for the Nicolai map in arbitrary gauges was developed in
[
13
]
and independently in
[
14
]
. This framework has potential applications in all kinds of supersymmetric theories, e.g. as recently investigated
[
15
]
in supermembrane and matrix theory.

We should highlight that the Nicolai map and its coupling flow operator are (depending on the theory) not necessarily unique. The original proof
[
1
,
2
]
by Nicolai only shows that there exists such a map. The non-uniqueness was seen most strikingly in
[
11
]
, where it was shown that there exist two distinct Nicolai maps in
{\cal N}=\,1

D=6
SYM (at least to third order in the coupling). In fact, one of the main results of this work is that in case of
{\cal N}=4
SYM, there is a 15-dimensional ambiguity in the coupling flow operator. However, by definition of the map, correlators are independent of the particular choice of the Nicolai map (or coupling flow operator).

To this date, most works on Nicolai maps were restricted to
\mathcal{N}=\,1
supersymmetry. Only in
[
9
]
a Nicolai map for
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM was deduced by dimensional reduction from the map for
\mathcal{N}=\,1

D=10
SYM. In our work, we develop a more extensive framework for dealing with
\mathcal{N}=\,4
supersymmetry
1
1

1

other theories with extended supersymmetry such as
\mathcal{N}=\,2

D=6
SYM could be described in an analogous fashion.
. Generally, we distinguish two possibilities for obtaining Nicolai maps in
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM. The first one is dimensional reduction from ten to four dimensions, while the second one makes use of an
\mathcal{N}=\,1
off-shell superfield formalism for
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM. The former approach treats all four supersymmetries on an equal footing, whereas the latter singles out one of the supersymmetries. This leads to an ambiguity in the coupling flow operator and the corresponding Nicolai map. We explain this by an analysis of the R-symmetry of
R_{g}
. Essentially, the operator is subject to
\mathrm{SU}(4)
R-symmetry transformations and to a principle of superpositions with weight one. This suggests a general description of the coupling flow operator in terms of an
\mathfrak{su}(4)
R-symmetry freedom that incorporates the two before-mentioned results as special points in the Lie algebra.

In Section
2
we compare two formulations of the
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM action and how they are actually equivalent. We start with the
\mathcal{N}=1
superfield formalism and then consider dimensional reduction from
\mathcal{N}=\,1

D=10
SYM to
\mathcal{N}=\,4

D=4
SYM. In Section
3
we compare the two corresponding formulations of the coupling flow operator and place them as special cases within a general R-symmetric understanding of the operator. In Section
4
we investigate the resulting Nicolai maps. Finally, in Section
5
we give our conclusions and an outlook to possible future directions that this work may point at.

Due to the technical nature of this paper, we present many of the calculations in detailed appendices
A
-
E
in order not to disrupt the common theme of our arguments.

1.2
Basics of the Nicolai map

To begin with, we recall the essentials of the Nicolai map, without specializing to a particular theory. Any supersymmetric theory can be expressed in terms of bosonic and fermionic (potentially including ghost) fields. Usually the latter appear quadratically so that they can be integrated out, giving a nonlocal functional determinant. The resulting action can be written as

S_{g}[\phi]\ =\ S_{g}^{\mathrm{b}}[\phi]+\hbar\ S_{g}^{\mathrm{f}}[\phi]\ ,

(1.1)

with coupling constant
g
and local, nonlocal parts of the action
S_{g}^{\mathrm{b}}
,
S_{g}^{\mathrm{f}}
respectively. Here,
\phi
stands for the bosonic field content of the theory, i.e. for
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM we have
\phi=(A_{\mu},\ \varphi_{i})
with the gauge field
A_{\mu}
and six real scalars
\varphi_{i}
. Expectation values
2
2

2

by the vanishing of the vacuum energy in supersymmetric theories, we have the normalization
\langle 1\rangle_{g}=1
.
of bosonic observables
X[\phi]
in the theory (
1.1
) are given by

\langle X[\phi]\rangle_{g}\ =\ \int\mathcal{D}\phi\ \exp\bigl\{{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}}S_{g}[\phi]\bigr\}\ X[\phi]\ .

(1.2)

The Nicolai map is a (nonlinear and nonlocal) field transformation

T_{g}:\ \phi(x)\ \mapsto\ \phi^{\prime}(x;\ g,\phi)\ ,

(1.3)

invertible at least as a formal power series in
g
, with the defining property

\langle X[\phi]\rangle_{g}\ =\ \langle X[T_{g}^{-1}\phi]\rangle_{0}\qquad\forall X\ ,

(1.4)

that connects the interacting theory at coupling
g
with the free theory (
g=0
). We stress again that the map
T_{g}
is not necessarily unique. However, since we construct all maps from the defining relation (
1.4
), correlators do not depend on the choice of the particular map.
Taking the derivative of (
1.4
) with respect to the coupling gives

\partial_{g}\langle X[\phi]\rangle_{g}\ =\ \langle(\partial_{g}+R_{g}[\phi])X[\phi]\rangle_{g}\ ,

(1.5)

which defines the infinitesimal version of the Nicolai map, the so-called coupling flow operator

R_{g}[\phi]\ =\ \int\!\mathrm{d}x\ \bigl(\partial_{g}T_{g}^{-1}\circ T_{g}\bigr)\phi(x)\,\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi(x)}\ =:\ \int\!\mathrm{d}x\ K[\phi;\;x]\,\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi(x)}\ ,

(1.6)

with kernel
K
. By setting
X[\phi]=T_{g}\phi
in (
1.4
), one can quickly derive
[
16
]
the relation

(\partial_{g}+R_{g}[\phi])T_{g}\phi\ =\ 0\ .

(1.7)

This is a well-known differential equation solved by the path-ordered exponential

T_{g}\,\phi\ =\ \overrightarrow{\cal P}\exp\Bigl\{-\!\int_{0}^{g}\!\mathrm{d}h\ R_{h}[\phi]\Bigr\}\ \phi\ ,

(1.8)

which was first found in
[
12
]
. This shows that the knowledge of
R_{g}
completely captures the analytic
g
-dependence of the Nicolai map, allowing its perturbative construction. It is the main objective of this work to find the explicit and most general form of
R_{g}
for
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM.

Next, we note the characteristic properties
3
3

3

which were originally used as the defining conditions of the Nicolai map, but can be traded for the single relation (
1.4
).
of the Nicolai map and the corresponding infinitesimal properties for the coupling flow operator. Writing (
1.4
) in terms of path integrals and collecting powers of
\hbar
, one finds

S^{\mathrm{b}}_{0}[T_{g}\phi]\ =\ S^{\mathrm{b}}_{g}[\phi]\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad S^{\mathrm{f}}_{0}[T_{g}\phi]-\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{tr}\ln{\textstyle\frac{\delta T_{g}\phi}{\delta\phi}}\ =\ S^{\mathrm{f}}_{g}[\phi]\ ,

(1.9)

the ‘free-action’ and ‘determinant-matching’ conditions respectively. For gauge theories, we have the additional property that the chosen gauge fixing function
\mathcal{G}(\phi)
is a fixed point of the Nicolai map. From (
1.5
), it is straightforward to deduce
[
16
]
the corresponding infinitesimal conditions

(\partial_{g}+R_{g}[\phi])S^{\mathrm{b}}_{g}[\phi]\ =\ 0\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad(\partial_{g}+R_{g}[\phi])S^{\mathrm{f}}_{g}[\phi]\ =\ \int\!\mathrm{d}x\ \frac{\delta K[\phi;\;x]}{\delta\phi(x)}\ ,

(1.10)

as well as the gauge condition

(\partial_{g}+R_{g}[\phi])\mathcal{G}(\phi)\ =\ 0\ .

(1.11)

For completeness, although it will not be relevant to the rest of this work, we include here a few general remarks on regularization and renormalization. Since the Nicolai map itself only consists of tree graphs, regularization is not required at this stage. Only in the end, when computing correlators in the free theory with (
1.4
), one has to contract trees with each other. This generates loops (but interestingly, none of them purely fermionic) that have to be regularized. In the case of
{\cal N}=\,1
SYM this technique and the subsequent renormalization are successfully carried out in the paper
[
7
]
from 1985, for example rederiving the universality of the gauge coupling to 1-loop order. The computational effort of this method as opposed to the traditional Feynman diagram approach is practically comparable.

1.3
Conventions and notation

In this paper, we work in four- and sometimes ten-dimensional Minkowski space equipped with the mostly plus metric

\eta^{\mu\nu}\ =\ \operatorname{diag}(-1,\ +1,\ +1,\ +1)\ ,\qquad\eta^{\Sigma\Theta}\ =\ \operatorname{diag}(-1,\ +1,\ ...\ ,\ +1)

(1.12)

respectively, where lowercase Greek indices run from 0 to 3 and uppercase Greek indices run from 0 to 9. For the four-dimensional spinor algebra, we adopt the conventions from
[
17
]
, including the chiral basis for the gamma matrices

\gamma^{\mu}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma^{\mu}\\
{\bar{\sigma}}^{\mu}&0\end{pmatrix}\ ,\qquad\gamma^{5}\ =\ \gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(1.13)

with sigma matrices

\sigma^{0}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\ ,\qquad\sigma^{1}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\ ,\qquad\sigma^{2}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma^{3}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(1.14)

and
{\bar{\sigma}}^{0}=\sigma^{0}
,
{\bar{\sigma}}^{1,2,3}=-\sigma^{1,2,3}
. The gamma matrices satisfy the Clifford algebra
\{\gamma^{\mu},\ \gamma^{\nu}\}=-2\eta^{\mu\nu}
.
In this basis, the chiral projectors
\mathrm{P}^{\pm}
take the form

\mathrm{P}^{+}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1+\mathrm{i}\gamma^{5})\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\ ,\qquad\mathrm{P}^{-}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1-\mathrm{i}\gamma^{5})\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\ .

(1.15)

We often use the standard Feynman slash notation

\not{a}\ =\ \gamma^{\mu}a_{\mu}\ ,

(1.16)

with the exception that the slashed script letters
\not{\mathscr{A}}
and
\not{\mathscr{D}}
have a related but distinct meaning that is defined in the main text. We additionally define the antisymmetric

\gamma^{\mu\nu}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}-\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\mu})

(1.17)

and generally antisymmetrize indices with weight one, indicated by square brackets, e.g.

a^{[\mu}b^{\nu]}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(a^{\mu}b^{\nu}-a^{\nu}b^{\mu})\ .

(1.18)

All of our fields are in the adjoint representation of the gauge group which we take to be
\mathrm{SU}(n_{\mathrm{c}})
with real antisymmetric structure constants

f^{abc}
such that

f^{abc}f^{abd}\ =\ n_{\mathrm{c}}\delta^{cd}\ ,

(1.19)

where color indices run from 1 to
n_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}-1
, which we often leave implicit. For example, we write the non-abelian field-strength tensor
F_{\mu\nu}
in two equivalent notations

F_{\mu\nu}\ =\ \partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}+gA_{\mu}\times A_{\nu}\qquad\iff\qquad F^{a}_{\mu\nu}\ =\ \partial_{\mu}A^{a}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A^{a}_{\mu}+gf^{abc}A^{b}_{\mu}A^{c}_{\nu}\ ,

(1.20)

and the covariant derivative

\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\ =\ \partial_{\mu}+gA_{\mu}\times\ \qquad\iff\qquad(\mathrm{D}_{\mu}...)^{a}\ =\ \partial_{\mu}(...)^{a}+gf^{abc}A^{b}_{\mu}(...)^{c}\ .

(1.21)

We sum over implicit color indices of products, e.g.

F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\ =\ F^{a}_{\mu\nu}F^{a\,\mu\nu}\ ,

(1.22)

except when we write an explicit cross product, e.g.

\varphi\ \psi{\times}\lambda\ =\ f^{abc}\varphi^{a}\psi^{b}\lambda^{c}\ .

(1.23)

Further, e.g. when writing down Nicolai maps to second order, we often adopt from section 4 of
[
10
]
the shorthand notations for multiplying quantities in color and position space. This means that all objects are multiplied as color matrices or vectors, and integration kernels are convoluted with insertions of bosonic fields
A_{\mu}
or
\varphi_{i}
for
\mu=0,1,2,3
and
i=1,...,6
. For example, we would write in two equivalent notations the expression

\partial^{\rho}C\varphi_{i}\partial_{\mu}CA_{\rho}{\times}\varphi_{i}\qquad\iff\qquad\int\mathrm{d}^{4}y\;\mathrm{d}^{4}z\ \partial^{\rho}C(x-y)\;(f^{abc}\varphi^{b}_{i})(y)\;\partial_{\mu}C(y-z)\;(f^{cde}A^{d}_{\rho})(z)\varphi^{e}_{i}(z)\ ,

(1.24)

with the scalar propagator
C=\Box^{-1}
. We often summarize the bosonic fields in the symbol

\mathscr{A}_{\Gamma}\ =\ (A_{\mu},\ \varphi_{i})\ .

(1.25)

An overview over the various types of indices to be used in the following can be found in Table
1
.

Lastly, we recall the definitions of the basic building blocks of the coupling flow operator in
\mathcal{N}=\,1

D=4
SYM
[
13
]
, since these also appear in the more complicated
\mathcal{N}=\,4
case. The free gaugino and ghost propagators
S_{0}
and
G_{0}
which will be used for the perturbative expansion of their full versions are given by

S_{0}\ =\ \not{\partial}^{\;-1}\ =\ -\not{\partial}C\ ,\qquad G_{0}\ =\ ({\textstyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}{\partial A_{\mu}}}\partial_{\mu})^{-1}\ ,

(1.26)

respectively with the gauge fixing function
\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
.
An object that appears often in the context of the Nicolai map is the free projector

\Pi{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\ =\ \delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}-\partial_{\mu}G_{0}{\textstyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}{\partial\mathscr{A}_{\nu}}}\ ,

(1.27)

which we often extend to capital greek indices

\Pi{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\ =\ \delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}-\partial_{\Gamma}G_{0}{\textstyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}{\partial\mathscr{A}_{\Sigma}}}\ ,

(1.28)

with the understanding that in the reduced four-dimensional theory
\partial_{3+i}=0
for
i=1,...,6
. Among all possible gauges, the Landau gauge
\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})=\partial^{\mu}A_{\mu}
takes on a special role, since in that case
G_{0}\equiv C
so that
\Pi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
equals the standard transversal projector

\amalg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\ =\ \delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}-\partial_{\mu}C\partial^{\nu}\ .

(1.29)

The latter splits the Yang–Mills fields into transversal and longitudinal components

A_{\mu}\ =\ A^{\mathrm{T}}_{\mu}+A^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mu}\ ,\qquad A^{\mathrm{T}}_{\mu}\ =\ \amalg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}A_{\nu}\ ,\qquad A^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mu}\ =\ (\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}-\amalg^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}})A_{\nu}\ =\ \partial_{\mu}C\ \partial\cdot A\ ,

(1.30)

where we abbreviate
\partial\cdot A=\partial^{\mu}A_{\mu}
. In arbitrary gauges (or outside of the gauge hypersurface of the Landau gauge), it is helpful to define the ‘conjugate’ Yang–Mills field

A^{*}_{\mu}\ :=\ A^{\mathrm{T}}_{\mu}-A^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mu}\ =\ A_{\mu}-2\partial_{\mu}C\partial\cdot A\ ,

(1.31)

although for the most part in this work, we restrict ourselves to the Landau gauge hypersurface, where
A=A^{*}
.

2
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM action

A common formulation of the
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM invariant action (without a topological term
4
4

4

which we neglect here for simplicity but could be included in a future analysis. It is expected to lead to an additional chiral freedom in our theory.
) is
[
18
]

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{inv}}\ =\ \int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\Bigl\{

\displaystyle-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\varphi_{i}\mathrm{D}^{\mu}\varphi_{i}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\bar{\chi}_{A}\not{\mathrm{D}}\mathrm{P}^{+}\chi^{A}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\bar{\tilde{\chi}}^{A}\not{\mathrm{D}}\mathrm{P}^{-}\tilde{\chi}_{A}

(2.1)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}g\ t^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63753pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45537pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{AB}}}\bar{\tilde{\chi}}^{A}\mathrm{P}^{+}\varphi_{i}\times\chi^{B}+\mathrm{i}g\ t^{{{iAB}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.32564pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.66116pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\bar{\chi}_{A}\mathrm{P}^{-}\varphi_{i}\times\tilde{\chi}_{B}-{\textstyle\frac{g^{2}}{4}}(\varphi_{i}\times\varphi_{j})^{2}\Bigr\}\ ,

in terms of Weyl spinors
\chi^{A}
,
\tilde{\chi}_{A}
where
\tilde{\chi}_{A}=\mathrm{C}(\bar{\chi}^{A})^{\mathrm{T}}
with the charge conjugation operator
\mathrm{C}
in four dimensions. All fields are in the adjoint representation of the gauge group, with color indices left implicit. Here,
\chi^{A}
transforms as a
\mathbf{4}
under the global
\mathrm{SU}(4)\ \cong\ \operatorname{SO}(6)
R-symmetry, while
\tilde{\chi}_{A}
transforms as a
\bar{\mathbf{4}}
. The indices
i=1,2,...,6
label the six bosonic fields
\varphi_{i}
that transform as a
\mathbf{6}
. Furthermore, the coefficients
t^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63753pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45537pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{AB}}}=(t^{{{iAB}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.32564pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.66116pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}})^{*}
are the structure constants of the R-symmetry, or in other words Clebsch-Gordon coefficients that couple two
\mathbf{4}
’s to a
\mathbf{6}

[
19
]
. They allow us to define anti-symmetric complex scalars

\varphi_{AB}\ =\ t^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63753pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45537pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{AB}}}\varphi_{i}\ ,\qquad\varphi^{AB}\ =\ t^{{{iAB}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.32564pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.66116pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\varphi_{i}\ =\ (\varphi_{AB})^{*}

(2.2)

and will be specified explicitly when we construct the action below.
In this work, we find it advantageous to work with Majorana spinors instead of Weyl spinors. To that aim, we define

\psi^{A}\ =\ \mathrm{P}^{+}\chi^{A}\ +\ \mathrm{P}^{-}\tilde{\chi}_{A}\ ,\qquad{\bar{\psi}}_{A}\ =\ \bar{\chi}_{A}\mathrm{P}^{-}\ +\ \bar{\tilde{\chi}}^{A}\mathrm{P}^{+}\ .

(2.3)

With
\mathrm{C}\gamma_{5}=\gamma_{5}\mathrm{C}
, it is straightforward to check that
\psi^{A}=\mathrm{C}(\bar{\psi}_{A})^{\mathrm{T}}
, which shows that
\psi^{A}
are indeed Majorana spinors. A slight complication with this definition is that we need to be careful with R-symmetry transformations, since
\psi^{A}
transforms neither as a
\mathbf{4}
nor a
\bar{\mathbf{4}}
. We emphasize this point, because in this Majorana formulation, the position of the R-symmetry indices does not indicate the transformation properties of the corresponding quantities. When translating objects from the Weyl formulation to the Majorana formulation, index positions on the two sides of the equation do not match up. Hence, one generally has to remember the R transformation properties from the Weyl formulation. However, the Majorana formulation allows us to write the action in the more compact form

S_{\mathrm{inv}}\ =\ \int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\Bigl\{-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\varphi_{i}\mathrm{D}^{\mu}\varphi_{i}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}{\bar{\psi}}_{A}(\not{\mathrm{D}}\delta^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}+g\Phi^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\times)\psi^{B}-{\textstyle\frac{g^{2}}{4}}(\varphi_{i}\times\varphi_{j})^{2}\Bigr\}\ ,

(2.4)

with

\Phi^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\ :=\ 2\bigl[t^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63753pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45537pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{AB}}}\mathrm{P}^{+}-t^{{{iAB}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.32564pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.66116pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\mathrm{P}^{-}\bigr]\varphi_{i}\ \equiv\ (c^{i})^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\varphi_{i}\ ,

(2.5)

where we have defined a matrix-valued field
\Phi^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}
that is obtained from the scalars
\varphi_{i}
through contraction with matrix-valued coefficients
(c^{i})^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}
.
We further often use the shorthand

\not{\mathscr{D}}{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}\ :=\ \not{\mathrm{D}}\delta^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}+g\Phi^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\times\ .

(2.6)

In the following two subsections, we show how the action (
2.4
) is obtained from an
\mathcal{N}=\,1
superfield formalism and from dimensional reduction, respectively.

2.1
\mathcal{N}=\,1
superfield formalism

It is well established that
\mathcal{N}=\,4
SYM does not have a formulation in which all four supersymmetries are realized off-shell. However, it is possible to single out one of the supersymmetries to construct an
\mathcal{N}=\,4
action using an
\mathcal{N}=\,1
superfield formalism
[
19
]
. The field content resides in one vector superfield
V
and three chiral superfields
\Phi_{I}

V\ =\ (A_{\mu},\ \lambda,\ \mathcal{D})\ ,\qquad\Phi_{I}\ =\ (\phi_{I},\ \psi_{I},\ F_{I})\quad\textrm{with}\quad I\ =\ 1,2,3\ .

(2.7)

All fields are in the adjoint representation of the gauge group. The propagating degrees of freedom are the vector field
A_{\mu}
, four Weyl- (or equivalently Majorana-) spinors
\psi^{A}
(
A=1,2,3,4
, with
\lambda=\psi^{4}
) and three complex scalars
\phi_{I}
. Further, there is one real scalar auxiliary field
\mathcal{D}
and three complex scalar auxiliary fields
F_{I}
. In terms of superfields, the
\mathcal{N}=\,4
Lagrangian density in Weyl notation is the last component
5
5

5

recall that
\theta^{2}\theta^{\alpha}=0
and
{\bar{\theta}}^{2}{\bar{\theta}}_{\dot{\alpha}}=0
.
of a superfield:

\mathcal{L}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{g^{2}n_{\mathrm{c}}}}\mathrm{tr}\Bigl[{\textstyle\frac{1}{16}}\bigl(W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}\bigr|_{\theta\theta}+\mathrm{h.c.}\bigr)\ +\ \mathrm{e}^{-2V}\Phi^{\dagger}_{I}\mathrm{e}^{2V}\Phi_{I}\bigr|_{\theta\theta{\bar{\theta}}{\bar{\theta}}}\ +\ \mathrm{i}{\textstyle\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3!}}\bigl(\epsilon_{IJK}\Phi_{I}[\Phi_{J},\Phi_{K}]\bigr|_{\theta\theta}+\mathrm{h.c.}\bigr)\Bigr]\ ,

(2.8)

where
...|_{\theta\theta}
denotes the
\theta\theta
-component of a given superfield and so on. The trace is over color space. We have also introduced the non-abelian supersymmetric field strength
W_{\alpha}
and its conjugate

W_{\alpha}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\bar{\mathrm{D}}\bar{\mathrm{D}}\mathrm{e}^{-2V}\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{2V}\ ,\qquad\bar{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{e}^{-2V}\bar{\mathrm{D}}^{\dot{\alpha}}\mathrm{e}^{2V}\ ,

(2.9)

in chiral superspace, with the superspace covariant derivatives
\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}
,
\bar{\mathrm{D}}^{\dot{\alpha}}
.
Note that the coupling only appears as an overall factor
1/g^{2}
in front of (
2.8
).
One recovers the usual dependence on the coupling by rescaling
V\ \rightarrow\ gV
and
\Phi_{I}\ \rightarrow\ g\Phi_{I}
. Given the various superspace expansions, it is straightforward to obtain the Lagrangian explicitly in terms of components. The details on the computations that lead to the following results can be found in Appendix
A
. We find for the Lagrangian in the Majorana basis

\displaystyle g^{2}\mathcal{L}\ =

\displaystyle-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\bar{\lambda}\gamma^{\mu}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\lambda+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\epsilon_{IJK}\bigl(F_{I}\phi_{J}{\times}\phi_{K}+F^{\dagger}_{I}\phi_{J}^{\dagger}{\times}\phi_{K}^{\dagger}\bigr)

(2.10)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\phi^{\dagger}_{I}\mathrm{D}^{\mu}\phi_{I}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}{\bar{\psi}}_{I}\gamma^{\mu}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\psi_{I}+F^{\dagger}_{I}F_{I}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\epsilon_{IJK}\bigl(\phi_{I}{\bar{\psi}}_{J}\mathrm{P}^{+}{\times}\psi_{K}+\phi^{\dagger}_{I}{\bar{\psi}}_{J}\mathrm{P}^{-}{\times}\psi_{K}\bigr)

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2}\bigl({\bar{\psi}}_{I}\mathrm{P}^{-}\lambda{\times}\phi_{I}+{\bar{\psi}}_{I}\mathrm{P}^{+}\lambda{\times}\phi^{\dagger}_{I}\bigr)-\mathrm{i}\phi_{I}^{\dagger}\mathcal{D}{\times}\phi_{I}\ ,

and for the supersymmetry transformations

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}\phi_{I}\ =\ \sqrt{2}({\bar{\psi}}_{I}\mathrm{P}^{+})_{\alpha}\ ,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}\phi^{\dagger}_{I}\ =\ \sqrt{2}({\bar{\psi}}_{I}\mathrm{P}^{-})_{\alpha}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(\mathrm{P}^{+}\psi_{I})_{\beta}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2}(\mathrm{P}^{+}\gamma^{\mu})_{\beta\alpha}(\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\phi_{I})-\sqrt{2}(\mathrm{P}^{+})_{\beta\alpha}F_{I}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}(\mathrm{P}^{-}\psi_{I})_{\beta}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2}(\mathrm{P}^{-}\gamma^{\mu})_{\beta\alpha}(\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\phi^{\dagger}_{I})-\sqrt{2}(\mathrm{P}^{-})_{\beta\alpha}F^{\dagger}_{I}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}F_{I}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2}(\mathrm{D}_{\mu}{\bar{\psi}}_{I\beta})(\gamma^{\mu}\mathrm{P}^{-})_{\beta\alpha}-2\phi_{I}{\times}(\bar{\lambda}\mathrm{P}^{-})_{\alpha}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}F^{\dagger}_{I}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2}(\mathrm{D}_{\mu}{\bar{\psi}}_{I\beta})(\gamma^{\mu}\mathrm{P}^{+})_{\beta\alpha}-2\phi_{I}^{\dagger}{\times}(\bar{\lambda}\mathrm{P}^{+})_{\alpha}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}A_{\nu}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}(\bar{\lambda}\gamma_{\nu})_{\alpha}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}\mathcal{D}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}(\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\bar{\lambda}_{\beta})(\gamma_{5}\gamma^{\mu})_{\beta\alpha}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}\lambda_{\beta}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\gamma^{\mu\nu})_{\beta\alpha}F_{\mu\nu}+\mathcal{D}(\gamma_{5})_{\beta\alpha}\ .

The decisive advantage of the superfield formalism is that we can deduce the penultimate component
6
6

6

that is, the components with one less power of
\theta
and
{\bar{\theta}}
than maximal for the respective contributions.
of the superfield in (
2.8
). It reads

\displaystyle\mathring{\Delta}_{\alpha}\ =

\displaystyle\textstyle{\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\Bigl\{-\mathcal{D}\gamma_{5}\lambda-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\lambda+2\epsilon_{IJK}\bigl[\mathrm{P}^{+}\psi_{I}\phi_{J}{\times}\phi_{K}+\mathrm{P}^{-}\psi_{I}\phi^{\dagger}_{J}{\times}\phi^{\dagger}_{K}\bigr]+2\mathrm{i}\gamma_{5}\phi_{I}^{\dagger}\lambda{\times}\phi_{I}

(2.12)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2}\bigl[\gamma^{\mu}\mathrm{P}^{-}\psi_{I}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\phi_{I}+\gamma^{\mu}\mathrm{P}^{+}\psi_{I}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\phi^{\dagger}_{I}\bigr]-\sqrt{2}\bigl[\mathrm{P}^{+}\psi_{I}F^{\dagger}_{I}+\mathrm{P}^{-}\psi_{I}F_{I}\bigr]\Bigr\}_{\alpha}\ .

The superfield structure now enables us to write the invariant action as a supervariation

S_{\mathrm{inv}}\ =\ \int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\ \mathcal{L}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2g^{2}}}\ \delta_{\alpha}\mathring{\Delta}_{\alpha}\ ,

(2.13)

which will be the central ingredient in the canonical construction of the coupling flow operator later on.
To find the on-shell invariant action, we first need to insert the equations of motion for the auxiliary fields

\mathcal{D}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}\phi^{\dagger}_{I}{\times}\phi_{I}\ ,\qquad F_{I}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\epsilon_{IJK}\phi^{\dagger}_{J}{\times}\phi^{\dagger}_{K}\ ,

(2.14)

resulting in

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{inv}}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{g^{2}}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\;\Bigl\{-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}-\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\phi^{\dagger}_{I}\mathrm{D}^{\mu}\phi_{I}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}{\bar{\psi}}_{A}\not{\mathrm{D}}\psi^{A}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\epsilon_{IJK}\bigl(\phi_{I}{\bar{\psi}}_{J}\mathrm{P}^{+}{\times}\psi_{K}+\phi^{\dagger}_{I}{\bar{\psi}}_{J}\mathrm{P}^{-}{\times}\psi_{K}\bigr)

(2.15)

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2}\bigl({\bar{\psi}}_{I}\mathrm{P}^{-}\lambda{\times}\phi_{I}+{\bar{\psi}}_{I}\mathrm{P}^{+}\lambda{\times}\phi^{\dagger}_{I}\bigr)+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\phi^{\dagger}_{I}\times\phi_{I})^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon_{IJK}\epsilon_{ILM}(\phi_{J}\times\phi_{K})(\phi^{\dagger}_{L}\times\phi^{\dagger}_{M})\Bigr\}\ .

Note that in this expression, the scalars are represented by three complex fields
\phi_{I}
. In order to get to the formulation (
2.4
), we need to replace these by six real fields
\varphi_{i}
by a suitable identification

\phi_{I}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}(\varphi_{I+3}+\mathrm{i}\varphi_{I})\ ,\qquad\phi_{I}^{\dagger}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}(\varphi_{I+3}-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{I})\ ,

(2.16)

giving

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{inv}}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{g^{2}}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\;\Bigl\{

\displaystyle-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\varphi_{i}\mathrm{D}^{\mu}\varphi_{i}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}{\bar{\psi}}_{A}\not{\mathrm{D}}\psi^{A}

(2.17)

\displaystyle+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{IJK}\bigl({\bar{\psi}}_{I}\varphi_{J+3}{\times}\psi_{K}-{\bar{\psi}}_{I}\varphi_{J}\gamma_{5}{\times}\psi_{K}\bigr)+{\bar{\psi}}_{I}\varphi_{I+3}{\times}\lambda+{\bar{\psi}}_{I}\varphi_{I}\gamma_{5}{\times}\lambda-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}(\varphi_{i}\times\varphi_{j})^{2}\Bigr\}\ ,

where for the potential term the Jacobi identity in color space was used. From this expression we can read off the coefficients
(c^{i})^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}
from (
2.5
)

(c^{I})^{{{J}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{4}}}\ =\ \mathrm{i}\delta_{IJ}\gamma_{5}\ ,\qquad(c^{I+3})^{{{J}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{4}}}\ =\ \mathrm{i}\delta_{IJ}\mathbbm{1}_{4}\ ,\qquad(c^{I})^{{{J}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{K}}}\ =\ \mathrm{i}\epsilon_{IJK}\gamma_{5}\ ,\qquad(c^{I+3})^{{{J}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{K}}}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}\epsilon_{IJK}\mathbbm{1}_{4}\ ,

(2.18)

wich are anti-symmetric under exchange of
A
and
B
and all others are zero. We will show in Section
2.2
that these exactly match the coefficients obtained from dimensional reduction.

To conclude this subsection, we note two results that will be used for the canonical construction of the coupling flow operator in Section
3.1
. The susy-transformations of the six real scalars are

\delta_{\alpha}\varphi_{i}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}{\bar{\psi}}_{J}(c^{i})^{{{J}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{4}}}\ ,

(2.19)

and the penultimate superfield component in terms of the six real scalars and with the auxiliary fields integrated out can be brought to the compact form

\Delta_{\alpha}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\Bigl\{-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\lambda-(\Phi^{{{4}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}})^{\dagger}\not{\mathscr{D}}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\psi^{B}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\Phi^{{{4}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}})^{\dagger}\Phi^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}{\times}\psi^{B}\Bigr\}\ .

(2.20)

2.2
Dimensional reduction

As an alternative to the construction via the
\mathcal{N}=\,1
superfield formalism, an on-shell action of
\mathcal{N}=\,4

D=4
SYM can be obtained by dimensional reduction from
\mathcal{N}=\,1

D=10
SYM
[
18
]

S^{(10)}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{g^{2}}}\int\mathrm{d}^{10}x\Bigl\{-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F^{\Sigma\Theta}F_{\Sigma\Theta}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\bar{\lambda}\ \Gamma^{\Sigma}\ \mathrm{D}_{\Sigma}\ \lambda\Bigr\}\ ,

(2.21)

where capital greek indices label the ten-dimensional representation of the Lorentz group and
\Gamma^{\Sigma}
are the ten-dimensional gamma matrices.
We reduce the gauge field as

A_{\Sigma}\ =\ (A_{\mu},\ \varphi_{i})\ ,

(2.22)

which leads to the reduction of the Yang–Mills term

-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F^{\Sigma\Theta}F_{\Sigma\Theta}\quad\longrightarrow\quad-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{\mu}\varphi_{i}\mathrm{D}^{\mu}\varphi_{i}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}(\varphi_{i}\times\varphi_{j})^{2}\ .

(2.23)

For the Dirac term, we write the gamma matrices as

\Gamma^{\mu}\ =\ \mathbbm{1}_{8}\otimes\gamma^{\mu}\ ,\qquad\Gamma^{AB}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}0&\rho^{AB}\\
\rho_{AB}&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes\mathrm{i}\gamma_{5}\ ,\qquad A,B\ =\ 1,2,3,4\ ,

(2.24)

with antisymmetric
4\times 4
-matrices

(\rho^{AB})_{CD}\ =\ \delta_{AC}\delta_{BD}-\delta_{AD}\delta_{BC}\ ,\qquad(\rho_{AB})_{CD}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon_{ABFG}(\rho^{FG})_{CD}\ =\ \epsilon_{ABCD}\ .

(2.25)

It is convenient to define antisymmetric

\varphi_{I4}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\varphi_{I}+\mathrm{i}\varphi_{I+3})\ ,\qquad\varphi^{AB}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon^{ABCD}\varphi_{CD}\ =\ (\varphi_{AB})^{*}\ .

(2.26)

In the literature one often finds a factor
{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}
in front of the first equation in (
2.26
). In order to match the coefficients from our previous analysis, we prefer to chose a normalization
{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}
instead. Note that this explicitly determines the Clebsch-Gordon coefficients
t^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63753pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45537pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{AB}}}
from (
2.2
) to be

\displaystyle(t^{I})_{J4}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\delta_{IJ}\ =\ (t^{I})^{J4}\ ,

\displaystyle(t^{I{+}3})_{J4}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\delta_{IJ}\ =\ -(t^{I{+}3})^{J4}\ ,

(2.27)

\displaystyle(t^{I})_{JK}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\epsilon_{IJK}\ =\ (t^{I})^{JK}\ ,

\displaystyle(t^{I{+}3})_{JK}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\epsilon_{IJK}\ =\ -(t^{I{+}3})^{JK}\ .

For a matching of the bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom, the spinor
\lambda
has to be a Majorana-Weyl-spinor, which can be realized in the structure

\lambda\ =\ (\mathrm{P}^{+}\chi^{1},\ldots,\mathrm{P}^{+}\chi^{4},\mathrm{P}^{-}\tilde{\chi}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{P}^{-}\tilde{\chi}_{4})^{\mathrm{T}}\ ,\quad\text{with}\quad\tilde{\chi}_{A}\ =\ \mathrm{C}\bar{\chi}^{A\;\mathrm{T}}\ ,

(2.28)

where
\mathrm{C}
is the charge conjugation operator in four dimensions. We find that the Dirac term becomes

-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\bar{\lambda}\ \Gamma^{\Sigma}\ \mathrm{D}_{\Sigma}\ \lambda\quad\longrightarrow\quad-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}{\bar{\psi}}_{A}\ \not{\mathscr{D}}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\ \psi^{B}\ ,

(2.29)

with the shorthand (
2.6
), the Majorana-spinors (
2.3
) and

\Phi^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\ =\ (c^{i})^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\varphi_{i}\ =\ \bigl[(\rho^{CD})_{AB}\mathrm{P}^{+}-(\rho_{CD})_{AB}\mathrm{P}^{-}\bigr]\varphi_{CD}\ =\ 2\bigl[t^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63753pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45537pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{AB}}}\mathrm{P}^{+}-t^{{{iAB}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.2262pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.32564pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.66116pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\mathrm{P}^{-}\bigr]\varphi_{i}\ .

(2.30)

It is easy to verify that the coefficients
(c^{i})^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}
defined this way are equivalent to those found in the superfield formalism (
2.18
).

3
Coupling flow operator

In this section we construct the coupling flow operator (
1.5
) first via the canonical construction
3.1
and then via dimensional reduction
3.2
. Reconciling the two approaches, we propose a unified framework for the operator in
3.3
.

3.1
From the canonical construction

The basic procedure of the following construction is exactly the same as in the
\mathcal{N}=\,1
case (see e.g.
[
13
]
). We merely have more fields to take into account.
In order to fix the redundant degrees of freedom of the gauge theory, following the Faddeev-Popov procedure we add a gauge fixing term
S_{\mathrm{gf}}
with gauge fixing function
\mathcal{G}(\tilde{\mathscr{A}})
and ghost fields
\widetilde{\bar{C}},\ \widetilde{C}
to the full action
S_{\textsc{SUSY}}
. We use the on-shell invariant action (
2.17
), so that

\displaystyle S_{\textsc{SUSY}}[\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{\psi},\widetilde{{\bar{\psi}}},\widetilde{C},\widetilde{\bar{C}}]\ =\ S_{\mathrm{inv}}[\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{\psi},\widetilde{{\bar{\psi}}}]\ +\ S_{\mathrm{gf}}[\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\varphi},\widetilde{C},\widetilde{\bar{C}}]\ ,

(3.1)

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{inv}}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{g^{2}}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\;\Bigl\{-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\widetilde{F}_{\mu\nu}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}_{\mu}\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}^{\mu}\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}-{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\widetilde{{\bar{\psi}}}_{A}\widetilde{\not{\mathscr{D}}}{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}\widetilde{\psi}^{B}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}(\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}\times\widetilde{\varphi}_{j})^{2}\Bigr\}\ ,

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{gf}}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{g^{2}}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\;\Bigl\{-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2\xi}}\mathcal{G}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\varphi})^{2}+g\,\widetilde{\bar{C}}{\textstyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\varphi})}{\partial\widetilde{A}_{\mu}}}\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}_{\mu}\widetilde{C}+g\,\widetilde{\bar{C}}{\textstyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\varphi})}{\partial\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}\times\widetilde{C}\Bigr\}\ ,

where we emphasize that the fields are in the (canonical or geometric) scaling where the coupling only occurs as an overall factor
1/g^{2}
(and one factor of
g
multiplying two ghost terms) by explicitly putting tildes on all scaled quantities. The usual dependence on the coupling is recovered after rescaling all fields with an appropriate power of
g
(i.e.
\widetilde{A}=gA
,
\widetilde{\varphi}=g\varphi
).
In this scaling we can write the
g
derivative of the action as a supervariation up to a Slavnov variation
7
7

7

strictly, this should be written with the auxiliary fields still present, but since we can integrate them out after the construction, we leave them implicit here.
,

\partial_{g}S_{\textsc{SUSY}}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{g^{3}}}\bigr\{\delta_{\alpha}\Delta_{\alpha}-\sqrt{g}\,s\,\Delta_{\mathrm{gh}}\bigr\}\ ,

(3.2)

with the superfield component

\Delta_{\alpha}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\Bigl\{-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{F}_{\mu\nu}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\widetilde{\lambda}-(\widetilde{\Phi}^{{{4}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}})^{\dagger}\not{\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\widetilde{\psi}^{B}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\widetilde{\Phi}^{{{4}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}})^{\dagger}\widetilde{\Phi}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}{\times}\widetilde{\psi}^{B}\Bigr\}\ ,

(3.3)

the ghost contribution

\Delta_{\mathrm{gh}}\ =\ \int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\;\;\Bigl\{\widetilde{\bar{C}}\ \mathcal{G}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\varphi})\Bigr\}\ ,

(3.4)

the supervariations (
2.11
) and the BRST (or Slavnov) variations

\displaystyle s\widetilde{A}_{\mu}\ =\ \sqrt{g}\;\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}_{\mu}\widetilde{C}\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle s\widetilde{\lambda}\ =\ \sqrt{g}\;\widetilde{\lambda}\times\widetilde{C}\ ,\qquad

\displaystyle s\widetilde{\bar{\lambda}}\ =\ \sqrt{g}\;\widetilde{\bar{\lambda}}\times\widetilde{C}\ ,\qquad

(3.5)

\displaystyle s\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}\ =\ \sqrt{g}\;\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}\times\widetilde{C}\ ,

\displaystyle s\widetilde{C}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{\sqrt{g}}{2}}\;\widetilde{C}\times\widetilde{C}\ ,

\displaystyle s\widetilde{\bar{C}}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{\xi}}\;\mathcal{G}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\varphi})\ ,

\displaystyle s\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}\ =\ \sqrt{g}\;\widetilde{\varphi}_{i}\times\widetilde{C}\ ,

\displaystyle s\widetilde{\psi}_{I}\ =\ \sqrt{g}\;\widetilde{\psi}_{I}\times\widetilde{C}\ ,

\displaystyle s\widetilde{F}\ =\ \sqrt{g}\;\widetilde{F}_{I}\times\widetilde{C}\ .

An intermediate scaled coupling flow operator is given by
[
4
,
6
]

\mathrm{tr}\bigl\{(\mathscr{C}_{\Sigma})^{{{4}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}S^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\not{\mathscr{A}}{{}^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.8526pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.75186pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}}}}\times\not{\mathscr{A}}{{}^{{{*C}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.1442pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.1442pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.3026pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.50186pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{4}}}}\bigr\}+\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{{\textstyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathscr{A}_{\Gamma}}}}}\Pi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptsc \mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.25696pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule -S^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(x,y;\mathscr{A})\ ,\qquad\not{\mathscr{D}}{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.8526pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.75186pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}}}}\;S^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.8526pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.75186pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(x,y;\mathscr{A})\ \delta^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\delta(x-y)\ ,

(3.10)

and

\mathrm{i}\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.93124pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=19.92432pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.93124pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=19.92432pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.11299pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=16.39775pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.6601pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=14.53519pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}C(x)\bar{C}(y)\ =\ G(x,y;\mathscr{A})\ ,\qquad{\textstyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}{\partial\mathscr{A}_{\Gamma}}}\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}\;G(x,y;\mathscr{A})\ =\ \delta(x-y)\ ,

(3.11)

respectively. Further we have

\not{\mathscr{A}}{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}\ =\ \mathscr{A}^{\Sigma}(\mathscr{C}_{\Sigma})^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\ =\ \not{A}\delta{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}+\Phi{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}\ ,\qquad\not{\mathscr{A}}^{*}{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}\ :=\ \not{A}^{*}\delta{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}+(\Phi{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}})^{\dagger}\ ,

(3.12)

where
A^{*}
is the conjugate gauge field (
1.31
). The second term in (
3.7
) also explicitly contains the longitudinal part of the gauge field
A_{\mu}^{\mathrm{L}}
(
1.30
). Lastly, we have the natural generalization (c.f.
[
13
]
) of the covariant projector

P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\ =\ \delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}-\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}G{\textstyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}{\partial\mathscr{A}_{\Sigma}}}\ ,

(3.13)

and its free version

\Pi^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\ =\ P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\bigr|_{g=0}\ .

(3.14)

3.2
From dimensional reduction

Since the ten-dimensional theory does not have an off-shell formalism, the operator cannot be constructed canonically in any gauge. However, for the Landau gauge, an expression for
R_{g}
was derived and shown to satisfy all necessary conditions in all the critical
[
18
]
dimensions
D=3,4,6,10

[
10
]
. Hence, in the following we restrict to the Landau gauge hypersurface, so there will be no distinction between
A
and
A^{*}
since
A^{\mathrm{L}}=0
. We start with the formula for the coupling flow operator in
\mathcal{N}=\,1

D=10
SYM:

R_{g}[\mathscr{A}]\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{32}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{{\textstyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathscr{A}_{\Gamma}}}}}{P^{(10)}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\ \mathrm{tr}^{(32)}\Bigl\{\Gamma_{\Sigma}S^{\mathrm{(10)}}\not{\mathscr{A}}\times\not{\mathscr{A}}\Bigl\}\ ,

(3.15)

where the trace is over
32\times 32
spinor space and

\not{\mathscr{A}}\ =\ \Gamma^{\Sigma}\mathscr{A}_{\Sigma}\ .

(3.16)

We now apply the same scheme for dimensional reduction that we have used to reduce the action in Section
2.2
.
It is easy to establish that
{P^{(10)}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\longrightarrow{P^{(4)}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94446pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.0625pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\Sigma}}}_{{{\Gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.70836pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5389pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.52777pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}
under dimensional reduction from ten to four dimensions. We can relate
S^{\mathrm{(10)}}
and
S^{\mathrm{(4)}}\equiv S
by dimensional reduction. When leaving out the superscript indicating the number of dimensions, we always mean the four-dimensional object. In ten dimensions, the
32\times 32
matrix
S^{\mathrm{(10)}}
is given by the contraction

S^{\mathrm{(10)}}\ =\ -\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.91667pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=5.41669pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.91667pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=5.41669pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.37154pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=4.87154pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.10071pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=4.60072pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}\lambda\bar{\lambda}\ .

(3.17)

With the dimensional reduction of the spinor
\lambda
(
2.28
) and our definition of the four-dimensional Majorana spinors (
2.3
), we can decompose the
32\times 32
matrix as

S^{\mathrm{(10)}}\ =\ \left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}(\mathrm{P}^{+}S{{}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}\mathrm{P}^{-})_{\alpha\beta}&(\mathrm{P}^{+}S{{}^{{{AB}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63753pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45537pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathrm{P}^{+})_{\alpha\beta}\\
\hline\cr(\mathrm{P}^{-}S{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.39691pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.63753pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.45537pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{AB}}}}\mathrm{P}^{-})_{\alpha\beta}&(\mathrm{P}^{-}S{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}_{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathrm{P}^{+})_{\alpha\beta}\end{array}\right)\ ,

(3.18)

where each block is a
16\times 16
matrix with ‘inner’ indices
A,\ B
and ‘outer’ indices
\alpha,\ \beta
, all ranging from one to four. Due to the chiral projectors, the position of the indices
A,\ B
matches the R-symmetry transformation properties, i.e. upper indices transform as a
\mathbf{4}
and lower indices as a
\bar{\mathbf{4}}
.
We can interpret
S^{\mathrm{(10)}}
as an
8\times 8
matrix with
4\times 4
matrix-valued entries and take a partial trace in the
8\times 8
matrix space so that we are left with a trace over
4\times 4
matrices (over the outer indices).
Using the representation of Gamma matrices (
2.24
) and bosonic fields (
2.26
), we further write
\not{\mathscr{A}}
in the same block notation as

\not{\mathscr{A}}\ =\ \Gamma^{\Sigma}\mathscr{A}_{\Sigma}\ =\ \left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\not{A}_{\alpha\beta}\delta^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}&(\mathrm{i}\gamma_{5})_{\alpha\beta}\varphi^{AB}\\
\hline\cr(\mathrm{i}\gamma_{5})_{\alpha\beta}\varphi_{AB}&\not{A}_{\alpha\beta}\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}_{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\end{array}\right)\ .

(3.19)

When multiplying two block matrices, we simply have to contract inner with inner indices and outer with outer indices.
This leads to

\not{\mathscr{A}}\times\not{\mathscr{A}}\ =\ \left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}(\not{A}\times\not{A})_{\alpha\beta}\delta^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}+(\mathbbm{1}_{4})_{\alpha\beta}\varphi^{AC}{\times}\varphi_{CB}&2(\not{A}\mathrm{i}\gamma_{5})_{\alpha\beta}\times\varphi^{AB}\\
\hline\cr 2(\mathrm{i}\gamma_{5}\not{A})_{\alpha\beta}\times\varphi_{AB}&(\not{A}\times\not{A})_{\alpha\beta}\delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}_{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}+(\mathbbm{1}_{4})_{\alpha\beta}\varphi_{AC}{\times}\varphi^{CB}\end{array}\right)\ .

(3.20)

In order to perform the partial trace, one multiplies the block matrices in (
3.15
) and then takes the trace over the blocks. For example, a simple contribution would be

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}^{\mathrm{(32)}}\ \Bigl\{\Gamma_{\mu}\ S^{\mathrm{(10)}}\ \mathbbm{1}_{8}\otimes(\not{A}\times\not{A})\Bigr\}

(3.21)

\displaystyle=\ \mathrm{tr}^{\mathrm{(32)}}\ \left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}(\gamma_{\mu})_{\alpha\gamma}(\mathrm{P}^{+}S^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\mathrm{P}^{-})_{\gamma\delta}(\not{A}\times\not{A})_{\delta\beta}&(\gamma_{\mu})_{\alpha\gamma}(\mathrm{P}^{+}S^{AB}\mathrm{P}^{+})_{\gamma\delta}(\not{A}\times\not{A})_{\delta\beta}\\
\hline\cr(\gamma_{\mu})_{\alpha\gamma}(\mathrm{P}^{-}S_{AB}\mathrm{P}^{-})_{\gamma\delta}(\not{A}\times\not{A})_{\delta\beta}&(\gamma_{\mu})_{\alpha\gamma}(\mathrm{P}^{-}S^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}_{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\mathrm{P}^{+})_{\gamma\delta}(\not{A}\times\not{A})_{\delta\beta}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\ \mathrm{tr}^{\mathrm{(4)}}\bigl\{\gamma_{\mu}\mathrm{P}^{+}S^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}\mathrm{P}^{-}\not{A}\times\not{A}\bigr\}\ +\ \mathrm{tr}^{\mathrm{(4)}}\bigl\{\gamma_{\mu}\mathrm{P}^{-}S^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}_{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}\mathrm{P}^{+}\not{A}\times\not{A}\bigr\}\ =\ \mathrm{tr}^{\mathrm{(4)}}\bigl\{\gamma_{\mu}S^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}\not{A}\times\not{A}\bigr\}\ ,

where in the last step we used the cyclicity of the trace to commute the chiral projectors. In the last step, since the positions of the indices of the gaugino propagator in the first vs. second term do not match up, the R-symmetry transformation properties become slightly nontransparent. This is a consequence of the way we defined our Majorana spinors (
2.3
). Step by step, one establishes the relation

\mathrm{tr}^{(32)}\Bigl\{\Gamma_{\Sigma}S^{\mathrm{(10)}}\not{\mathscr{A}}\times\not{\mathscr{A}}\Bigl\}\ =\ \mathrm{tr}^{(4)}\Bigl\{({\mathscr{C}_{\Sigma}})^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}S{{}^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.8526pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.75186pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}}}}\not{\mathscr{A}}{{}^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.22598pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.8526pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.75186pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}}\times\not{\mathscr{A}}^{*}{{}^{{{D}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.75377pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.1929pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.99492pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}}\Bigl\}\ ,

(3.22)

with the same definitions (
3.8
), (
3.9
), (
3.12
) as in the canonical construction.
Although the R-symmetry indices in (
3.22
) cannot be strictly assigned to
\mathbf{4}
’s or
\bar{\mathbf{4}}
’s, by construction through the above dimensional reduction, this object is invariant under R-symmetry transformations for
\Sigma=\mu
and transforms as a
\mathbf{6}
of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
for
\Sigma=3+i
.
